Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, um… I guess the main thing is I just can’t stay on track. Like, I’ll sit down to do something I actually need to do, like filling out an application or trying to finish something online, and it’s like my attention gets yanked away by the smallest thing.

A car goes by outside, or my phone lights up, or sometimes it’s not even something outside — it’s just some random thought that pops into my head, and suddenly I’m following that instead. Then I kind of come to and realize I haven’t been doing the thing I meant to do for, I don’t know, several minutes or longer. It’s frustrating because it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing to stop; it just happens before I even notice.

Yeah, definitely. I mean, looking back, it was there pretty early, like even in elementary school. I remember teachers moving my seat around a lot — like away from certain kids, or closer to the front, or right next to their desk — because I’d talk out of turn or get pulled into talking when I wasn’t supposed to.

And homework was a big thing too. I’d forget to bring it home, or I’d do it and forget to turn it in, or it would just disappear into my backpack somehow. I also remember rushing through worksheets just to be done, and then I’d get them back with all these careless mistakes where I clearly knew the material but skipped parts or didn’t read the directions right. So, yeah, it wasn’t just an adult thing. It was kind of a pattern even when I was little.

Then I kind of come to and realize I haven’t been doing the thing I meant to do for, I don’t know, several minutes or longer. I mean, looking back, it was there pretty early, like even in elementary school. I’d forget to bring it home, or I’d do it and forget to turn it in, or it would just disappear into my backpack somehow. Yeah, it does. The biggest place it’s messing me up is with trying to move forward career-wise. It’s really frustrating because it makes me look like I don’t care or I’m not serious, but I do care. I just keep losing track of the boring but important parts, and then suddenly I’m behind again.

head and I say it before they’re done talking.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, teachers were always moving my seat because I talked out of turn, forgot homework, and rushed through worksheets with careless mistakes.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve missed training deadlines and had to redo applications enough that I’m still not moving forward toward becoming an automotive technician, even though I know I can handle the hands-on work.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 10 months\n  manifestation: It’s been this disruptive for about 1 year and 10 months, History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess the main thing is I just can’t stay on track. Like, I’ll sit down to do something I actually need to do, like filling out an application or trying to finish something online, and it’s like my attention gets yanked away by the smallest thing.\n\nA car goes by outside, or my phone lights up, or sometimes it’s not even something outside — it’s just some random thought that pops into my head, and suddenly I’m following that instead. Then I kind of come to and realize I haven’t been doing the thing I meant to do for, I don’t know, several minutes or longer. It’s frustrating because it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing to stop; it just happens before I even notice.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, looking back, it was there pretty early, like even in elementary school. I remember teachers moving my seat around a lot — like away from certain kids, or closer to the front, or right next to their desk — because I’d talk out of turn or get pulled into talking when I wasn’t supposed to.\n\nAnd homework was a big thing too. I’d forget to bring it home, or I’d do it and forget to turn it in, or it would just disappear into my backpack somehow. I also remember rushing through worksheets just to be done, and then I’d get them back with all these careless mistakes where I clearly knew the material but skipped parts or didn’t read the directions right. So, yeah, it wasn’t just an adult thing. The biggest place it’s messing me up is with trying to move forward career-wise. Like, when I’m actually around cars and doing something physical, I feel like I can handle that.\n\nBut the steps to get there — applications, training stuff, deadlines, forms, online modules, whatever — that’s where I keep screwing it up. I’ve missed training deadlines, or I’ll start an application and not finish it, or I’ll submit something wrong and then have to redo it. And it’s happened enough times that I’m basically still stuck in the same place, even though I don’t feel like the actual work is beyond me.\n\nIt’s really frustrating because it makes me look like I don’t care or I’m not serious, but I do care. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I wouldn’t say it’s only when I’m feeling depressed. I mean, if I’m in a bad mood or frustrated, it probably feels worse because I have less patience for it, but the attention drifting happens even when I’m not especially down.

Like, I’ll be trying to do something pretty straightforward, like work on an application, and then it’s just… anything can pull me off. A car outside, my phone lighting up, some random thought like “oh, I should look that up,” and before I even realize it I’m not doing the application anymore. It’s not like I sit there thinking, “I’m too sad to focus.” It’s more like my brain just grabs onto whatever popped up and runs with it.

So, yeah, it can happen regardless of my mood. The main thing is I get knocked off track really easily by little sounds or thoughts, and I don’t always catch it until I’ve already lost time.

I just keep losing track of the boring but important parts, and then suddenly I’m behind again.\n\nDoctor: When your attention drifts like this, is it mostly only when you’re feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say it’s only when I’m feeling depressed. I mean, if I’m in a bad mood or frustrated, it probably feels worse because I have less patience for it, but the attention drifting happens even when I’m not especially down.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to do something pretty straightforward, like work on an application, and then it’s just… anything can pull me off. A car outside, my phone lighting up, some random thought like “oh, I should look that up,” and before I even realize it I’m not doing the application anymore. It’s not like I sit there thinking, “I’m too sad to focus.” It’s more like my brain just grabs onto whatever popped up and runs with it.\n\nSo, yeah, it can happen regardless of my mood. The main thing is I get knocked off track really easily by little sounds or thoughts, and I don’t always catch it until I’ve already lost time.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying and find it hard to stop the worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I worry about stuff like anybody would, especially when there’s something I’ve messed up or something I know I need to handle, but I don’t know if I’d say I spend a lot of time worrying in the sense of just sitting there worrying all day and not being able to stop.\n\nIt’s more like thoughts pop up and I can get stuck on them for a bit, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s “worry” exactly or just me thinking about things I need to do. I guess I’d need you to explain what you mean a little more, because I don’t want to say yes if that’s not really what’s happening.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say the thoughts that pull your attention away are usually worries that feel hard to control?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / no_occupation / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Distractibility\nmanifestation: \"The main thing is I get pulled off track by every little sound or thought, like I’ll be trying to work on an application and then a car outside, my phone, or some random idea sends me somewhere else before I even notice.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not usually worries, no. I mean, sometimes it can be something I need to handle, but most of the time it’s not like this big anxious thought that I can’t control. It’s more random than that.

Like I’ll be trying to work on an application, and then I hear a car outside and I’m thinking about what kind it is, or my phone lights up and I check it, or some random idea pops in my head like, “Oh, I should look that up real quick,” and then suddenly I’m off doing something else. And I don’t even notice right away that I stopped doing the thing I was supposed to be doing.

So I wouldn’t describe it as worry pulling me away most of the time. It’s more like every little sound or thought can grab my attention and send me somewhere else before I catch it.

It’s more random than that.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to work on an application, and then I hear a car outside and I’m thinking about what kind it is, or my phone lights up and I check it, or some random idea pops in my head like, “Oh, I should look that up real quick,” and then suddenly I’m off doing something else. And I don’t even notice right away that I stopped doing the thing I was supposed to be doing.\n\nSo I wouldn’t describe it as worry pulling me away most of the time. It’s more like every little sound or thought can grab my attention and send me somewhere else before I catch it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often act or speak before thinking?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / no_occupation / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Interrupting_Intruding\nmanifestation: \"I cut people off a lot, especially my mom or the counselor, because an answer pops into my head and I say it before they’re done talking.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. Probably more than I realize in the moment.

I cut people off a lot, especially my mom, and with my counselor too sometimes. It’s like they’ll be halfway through saying something, and an answer or a reaction pops into my head, and I just say it before they’re even done. I don’t usually mean it in a rude way, like I’m not trying to talk over them or dismiss what they’re saying, but it comes out fast because I feel like if I don’t say it right then, I’ll lose it or the moment will pass.

Then sometimes I catch myself and I’m like, “Sorry, go ahead,” because I realize I jumped in. My mom has definitely pointed it out before. With the counselor it’s more embarrassing, because I know I’m supposed to be listening, but I’ll still blurt something out before they finish the question. So, yeah, acting or speaking before thinking is definitely something that happens.

Like, I’ll sit down to do something I actually need to do, like filling out an application or trying to finish something online, and it’s like my attention gets yanked away by the smallest thing.\n\nA car goes by outside, or my phone lights up, or sometimes it’s not even something outside — it’s just some random thought that pops into my head, and suddenly I’m following that instead. Then I kind of come to and realize I haven’t been doing the thing I meant to do for, I don’t know, several minutes or longer. It’s frustrating because it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing to stop; it just happens before I even notice.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, looking back, it was there pretty early, like even in elementary school. So, yeah, it wasn’t just an adult thing. It was kind of a pattern even when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention problems cause trouble for you in more than one part of your life, like work, home, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. The biggest place it’s messing me up is with trying to move forward career-wise. I’m not working right now, and I’ve been trying to get on track toward becoming an automotive technician, because the hands-on part makes sense to me. Like, when I’m actually around cars and doing something physical, I feel like I can handle that.\n\nBut the steps to get there — applications, training stuff, deadlines, forms, online modules, whatever — that’s where I keep screwing it up. I’ve missed training deadlines, or I’ll start an application and not finish it, or I’ll submit something wrong and then have to redo it. And it’s happened enough times that I’m basically still stuck in the same place, even though I don’t feel like the actual work is beyond me.\n\nIt’s really frustrating because it makes me look like I don’t care or I’m not serious, but I do care. I just keep losing track of the boring but important parts, and then suddenly I’m behind again.\n\nDoctor: When your attention drifts like this, is it mostly only when you’re feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say it’s only when I’m feeling depressed. I mean, if I’m in a bad mood or frustrated, it probably feels worse because I have less patience for it, but the attention drifting happens even when I’m not especially down.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to do something pretty straightforward, like work on an application, and then it’s just… anything can pull me off. And I don’t even notice right away that I stopped doing the thing I was supposed to be doing.\n\nSo I wouldn’t describe it as worry pulling me away most of the time. It’s more like every little sound or thought can grab my attention and send me somewhere else before I catch it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act or speak before thinking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Probably more than I realize in the moment.\n\nI cut people off a lot, especially my mom, and with my counselor too sometimes. It’s like they’ll be halfway through saying something, and an answer or a reaction pops into my head, and I just say it before they’re even done. I don’t usually mean it in a rude way, like I’m not trying to talk over them or dismiss what they’re saying, but it comes out fast because I feel like if I don’t say it right then, I’ll lose it or the moment will pass.\n\nThen sometimes I catch myself and I’m like, “Sorry, go ahead,” because I realize I jumped in. My mom has definitely pointed it out before. With the counselor it’s more embarrassing, because I know I’m supposed to be listening, but I’ll still blurt something out before they finish the question. So, yeah, acting or speaking before thinking is definitely something that happens.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel physically restless, like it’s hard to sit still?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / no_occupation / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Feeling_Restless\nmanifestation: \"Since I can’t play soccer like before, sitting around the house makes me feel like I’m crawling out of my skin and I end up pacing from room to room.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. I feel that a lot, especially now that I’m not able to play soccer like I used to.

When I’m just sitting around the house, it gets really uncomfortable, like I’m not just bored, it’s more like my body feels irritated or wound up. I don’t know how to explain it exactly, but it’s like I’m crawling out of my skin if I’m sitting too long. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to relax or watch something, but then I’m shifting around, getting up, sitting back down, getting up again.

A lot of times I end up pacing from room to room without even really having a reason. Like I’ll walk into the kitchen, then back to the living room, then maybe to my room, and I’m not actually doing anything important. I just feel like I need to move. Soccer used to take a lot of that out of me, I think, so without that, being stuck at home makes it way more noticeable.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I did. Looking back, it was pretty much there even in elementary school.

I remember teachers moving my seat around a lot, like putting me closer to them or away from certain kids, because I’d talk out of turn or get distracted and start bothering whoever was near me. It wasn’t always me literally running around the room or anything, but sitting still and just quietly doing the work was hard. I’d rush through worksheets too, just trying to get them done, and then I’d make these careless mistakes that I probably could’ve avoided if I’d slowed down.

And homework was a whole thing. I forgot it all the time — either I didn’t bring it home, or I did it and didn’t turn it in, or I just completely forgot it existed until the teacher asked for it. So, yeah, school was kind of full of that stuff. Teachers were always adjusting where I sat or trying to keep me on track. At the time I think everyone just treated it like I was being careless or not trying hard enough, but it definitely wasn’t new as an adult.

      "Distractibility not primarily driven by uncontrollable worry"
    ]
  }
}

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"None\",\"name\":\"None\",\"description\":\"None\",\"reason\":\"The doctor is asking about excessive, hard-to-control worry, which relates to anxiety rather than any ADHD symptom item listed in the profile.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }

Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYeah, I do. Probably more than I realize in the moment.\n\nI cut people off a lot, especially my mom, and with my counselor too sometimes. It’s like they’ll be halfway through saying something, and an answer or a reaction pops into my head, and I just say it before they’re even done. I don’t usually mean it in a rude way, like I’m not trying to talk over them or dismiss what they’re saying, but it comes out fast because I feel like if I don’t say it right then, I’ll lose it or the moment will pass.\n\nThen sometimes I catch myself and I’m like, “Sorry, go ahead,” because I realize I jumped in. My mom has definitely pointed it out before.   }
